=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
For strings S and T consisting of lowercase English letters, and a string X consisting of 0 and 1, define the string f(S,T,X) consisting of lowercase English letters as follows:

- Starting with an empty string, for each i=1,2,\dots,|X|, append S to the end if the i-th character of X is 0, and append T to the end if it is 1.

You are given a string S consisting of lowercase English letters, and strings X and Y consisting of 0 and 1.
Determine if there exists a string T (which can be empty) such that f(S,T,X)=f(S,T,Y).
You have t test cases to solve.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
t
\mathrm{case}_1
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_t

Each case is given in the following format:
S
X
Y

Output

Print t lines. The i-th line should contain Yes if there exists a T that satisfies the condition for the i-th test case, and No otherwise.

Constraints


- 1 \leq t \leq 5 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq |S| \leq 5\times 10^5
- 1 \leq |X|,|Y| \leq 5\times 10^5
- S is a string consisting of lowercase English letters.
- X and Y are strings consisting of 0 and 1.
- The sum of |S| across all test cases in a single input is at most 5 \times 10^5.
- The sum of |X| across all test cases in a single input is at most 5 \times 10^5.
- The sum of |Y| across all test cases in a single input is at most 5 \times 10^5.

Sample Input 1

3
araara
01
111
araaaa
100100
0010111
abacabac
0
1111

Sample Output 1

Yes
No
No

Below, string concatenation is represented using +.
For the 1st test case, if T=ara, then f(S,T,X)=S+T=araaraara and f(S,T,Y)=T+T+T=araaraara, so f(S,T,X)=f(S,T,Y).
For the 2nd and 3rd test cases, there is no T that satisfies the condition.

Sample Input 2

2
empty
10101
00
empty
11111
111

Sample Output 2

Yes
Yes

T can be empty.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Compute a for S:
def exponent_of_primitive_root(s):
    n=len(s)
    pi=[0]*n
    for i in range(1,n):
        j=pi[i-1]
        while j>0 and s[i]!=s[j]:
            j=pi[j-1]
        if s[i]==s[j]:
            j+=1
        pi[i]=j
    p=n-pi[-1]
    if n%p==0:
        return n//p
    else:
        return 1
Wait if n=1, pi=[0], p=1, n%p=0, return1. Good.

Let's think about the proof of the "exponent equation" more carefully: If S=P^a and T=P^b, then f(S,T,X) = concatenation of blocks each equal to P^a for 0 and P^b for 1. So equation.

Let's maybe prove defect theorem for two words in a simple way to reassure:
Suppose A and B are nonempty words and there exist distinct words U,V over {A,B} with U=V. Consider the "free product" or use induction on |U|+|V|. If U and V start with same letter, cancel it. Eventually they start with different letters. WLOG U starts with A, V starts with B. Then A is a prefix of B or B prefix of A. If A is a proper prefix of B, then B = A B' and after cancellation we get a shorter relation. This process yields that A and B are powers of a common word. This is essentially the Nielsen cancellation proof. So yes.

Need maybe include a function to compute a:
def get_a(s):
    n = len(s)
    pi = [0]*n
    for i in range(1,n):
        j = pi[i-1]
        while j and s[i] != s[j]:
            j = pi[j-1]
        if s[i] == s[j]:
            j += 1
        pi[i] = j
    p = n - pi[-1]
    return n // p if n % p == 0 else 1
But if n=0? not possible.

Now, let's maybe derive an alternative proof to ensure no hidden assumption: The defect theorem says if two words A,B satisfy a nontrivial relation, then they are powers of a common word. But our relation is f(S,T,X)=f(S,T,Y). If X and Y are distinct, the relation is nontrivial. However, what if X and Y are distinct but the two products are identical as words over {S,T} because of associativity? No, a word over alphabet {S,T} is just a sequence of symbols; X and Y are exactly those sequences (0 for S, 1 for T). If X≠Y, sequences differ. So nontrivial.

Let's prove defect theorem for two words quickly:
Let A,B be nonempty words and let U,V be distinct words over {A,B} with U=V. Choose such a relation with minimal total length. If U,V start with same symbol, remove it to get a shorter relation, contradiction. So they start with different symbols. WLOG U starts with A, V starts with B. Then one of A,B is a prefix of the other. If neither is prefix, impossible. Suppose A is a proper prefix of B: B = A C. Then U = A U', V = A C V'. Cancel A: U' = C V'. This is a relation between A,B? Wait C is a suffix of B, not necessarily A or B. The standard proof uses "interchange" to reduce to a relation involving A and C, not A and B. It eventually shows A,B are powers of a common word. This is nontrivial but known. We don't need to implement proof.

But let's be absolutely certain the defect theorem applies to two words that are not necessarily primitive and one may be empty. For nonempty, yes. For empty, if T empty, the set {S, empty} has rank 1 because empty is the empty product; so conclusion holds. If S nonempty and T empty, they are powers of S (T=S^0). Good.

Now, let's think about possible issue: The equation f(S,T,X)=f(S,T,Y) is a relation between S and T, but T is the unknown. Let's test with a known relation between two words that are not powers of common word? I recall the "defect theorem" for n=2: If two words satisfy a nontrivial equation, then they are powers of a common word. Is this actually true? Let's search memory: The defect theorem states: If a set of n words X_1,...,X_n satisfies a nontrivial relation, then the rank of the set is at most n-1. For n=2, rank≤1. Yes. But wait, rank≤1 means there exists a word W such that each X_i is a product of powers of W? Actually rank 1 means each X_i is a power of the same word W. Yes. So true.

Let's try to find a counterexample to the theorem: A="ab", B="aba". Is there any relation? Let's use the theorem: if there were, rank≤1, so A and B powers of common word. But lengths 2 and 3, common word length must divide gcd(2,3)=1, so common word single char, but A has two different chars, impossible. Thus no relation. Good.
A="ab", B="ba". Lengths 2,2, common word length 1 or 2. If length1, both single char, no. If length2, common word must be "ab" and "ba" both powers of same length2 word; only possible if equal. So no relation. Indeed no relation.
A="abc", B="abca". Lengths 3,4, gcd1, common word single char, impossible. No relation.
A="abab", B="ab". Lengths 4,2, common word "ab", yes relation A=B^2. There are relations.
A="ababa", B="ab". Lengths 5,2, gcd1, common word single char? But A has two chars, impossible. So no relation. Could there be relation like A B = B A? "ababaab" vs "abababa", no. Good.

Thus theorem is strong.

Implementation with data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split():
it = iter(data)
t = int(next(it))
out = []
for _ in range(t):
    S = next(it)
    X = next(it)
    Y = next(it)
    a = get_a(S)
    zx = X.count(b'0')
    ox = len(X) - zx
    zy = Y.count(b'0')
    oy = len(Y) - zy
    dz = zx - zy
    do = ox - oy
    if do == 0:
        if dz == 0:
            out.append("Yes")
        else:
            out.append("No")
    else:
        num = -a * dz
        if num % do == 0:
            b = num // do
            if b >= 0:
                out.append("Yes")
            else:
                out.append("No")
        else:
            out.append("No")
sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

But wait, if do negative, num // do for divisible negative/negative gives positive. Example num=-2, do=-2 -> 1. Good. num=2, do=-2 -> -1. b negative -> No. This corresponds to b = -a dz / do. If do negative and dz negative, num positive? Let's check equation a dz + b do =0. If do<0, b = -a dz / do. For b positive, need dz and do same sign? Suppose X has more zeros and fewer ones? Let's not worry.

Let's test prefix function implementation on bytes:
def get_a(s):
    n = len(s)
    pi = [0]*n
    for i in range(1,n):
        j = pi[i-1]
        while j > 0 and s[i] != s[j]:
            j = pi[j-1]
        if s[i] == s[j]:
            j += 1
        pi[i] = j
    p = n - pi[-1]
    if n % p == 0:
        return n // p
    return 1
For n=1, pi[-1]=0, p=1, n%p=0, return1. Good.

Potential issue: If S is bytes, s[j] is int, s[i] int. Good.

Let's maybe write the code:

import sys

def primitive_exponent(s):
    n = len(s)
    p = (s + s).find(s, 1)
    if n % p == 0:
        return n // p
    return 1

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    t = int(data[0])
    out = []
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(t):
        S = data[idx]; X = data[idx+1]; Y = data[idx+2]; idx += 3
        a = primitive_exponent(S)
        zx = X.count(b'0')
        ox = len(X) - zx
        zy = Y.count(b'0')
        oy = len(Y) - zy
        num = a * (zy - zx)
        den = ox - oy
        if den == 0:
            if num == 0:
                out.append("Yes")
            else:
                out.append("No")
        else:
            if num % den == 0:
                b = num // den
                if b >= 0:
                    out.append("Yes")
                else:
                    out.append("No")
            else:
                out.append("No")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's write final code with some micro-optimizations:
def main():
    import sys
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data: return
    t = int(data[0])
    out = []
    append = out.append
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(t):
        S = data[idx]; X = data[idx+1]; Y = data[idx+2]; idx += 3
        n = len(S)
        if n == 1:
            a = 1
        else:
            p = (S + S).find(S, 1)
            a = n // p if n % p == 0 else 1
        zx = X.count(b'0')
        ox = len(X) - zx
        zy = Y.count(b'0')
        oy = len(Y) - zy
        num = a * (zy - zx)
        den = ox - oy
        if den == 0:
            append("Yes" if num == 0 else "No")
        else:
            if num % den == 0:
                b = num // den
                append("Yes" if b >= 0 else "No")
            else:
                append("No")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

Let's design a fast scanner:

    nonlocal? In Python, we can implement as a class or closure with index list. Simpler:
def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
    idx = 0
    L = len(data)
    def next_token():
        nonlocal idx
        while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
            idx += 1
        start = idx
        while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
            idx += 1
        return data[start:idx]

Let's compare speed: `read().split()` is generally faster than 1.5e6 `readline` calls. But 1.5e6 is not huge. The total sum lengths 5e5, but t=5e5, so many lines. 1.5e6 readline calls might be okay within 2s? Let's use `readline` to avoid memory overhead. But note: The first line t, then for each case S, X, Y. If there are Windows line endings, strip removes \r\n. Good.

Let's write code with `readline`:

import sys

def primitive_exponent(s):
    n = len(s)
    if n == 1:
        return 1
    p = (s + s).find(s, 1)
    if n % p == 0:
        return n // p
    return 1

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    t_line = input()
    if not t_line:
        return
    t = int(t_line)
    out = []
    append = out.append
    for _ in range(t):
        S = input().strip()
        X = input().strip()
        Y = input().strip()
        a = primitive_exponent(S)
        zx = X.count(b'0')
        ox = len(X) - zx
        zy = Y.count(b'0')
        oy = len(Y) - zy
        num = a * (zy - zx)
        den = ox - oy
        if den == 0:
            append("Yes" if num == 0 else "No")
        else:
            if num % den == 0:
                b = num // den
                append("Yes" if b >= 0 else "No")
            else:
                append("No")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

This is clean.
